=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given an integer array nums. A good subsequence is defined as a subsequence of nums where the absolute difference between any two consecutive elements in the subsequence is exactly 1.
Return the sum of all possible good subsequences of nums.
Since the answer may be very large, return it modulo 10^9 + 7.
Note that a subsequence of size 1 is considered good by definition.
 
Example 1:

Input: nums = [1,2,1]
Output: 14
Explanation:

Good subsequences are: [1], [2], [1], [1,2], [2,1], [1,2,1].
The sum of elements in these subsequences is 14.


Example 2:

Input: nums = [3,4,5]
Output: 40
Explanation:

Good subsequences are: [3], [4], [5], [3,4], [4,5], [3,4,5].
The sum of elements in these subsequences is 40.


 
Constraints:

1 <= nums.length <= 10^5
0 <= nums[i] <= 10^5

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def sumOfGoodSubsequences(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

- extend any good subsequence ending at previous position j < i with value v-1 or v+1 (because absolute diff exactly 1), by appending nums[i]=v. It contributes v to sum for each such subsequence, plus existing sum of subsequence.

Need maintain for each value x:
- cnt[x] = number of good subsequences ending at some processed index with last value x.
- sum[x] = total sum of elements over all good subsequences ending at some processed index with last value x.
When processing nums[i]=v:

- singleton: count 1, sum v.

Then add new_sum to total answer. Then update cnt[v] += new_count, sum[v] += new_sum (mod). Because subsequences ending at current index are distinct from previous ending at earlier positions with same value; adding counts.

Now, the final code can be:
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def sumOfGoodSubsequences(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        MOD = 10**9 + 7
        max_val = max(nums)
        size = max_val + 3
        cnt = [0] * size
        total = [0] * size
        ans = 0
        for v in nums:
            idx = v + 1
            prev_cnt = cnt[idx - 1] + cnt[idx + 1]
            prev_sum = total[idx - 1] + total[idx + 1]
            new_cnt = (1 + prev_cnt) % MOD
            new_sum = (v + prev_sum + v * prev_cnt) % MOD
            ans = (ans + new_sum) % MOD
            cnt[idx] = (cnt[idx] + new_cnt) % MOD
            total[idx] = (total[idx] + new_sum) % MOD
        return ans

Let's ensure code is syntactically correct:
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def sumOfGoodSubsequences(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        MOD = 10**9 + 7
        max_val = max(nums)
        size = max_val + 3
        cnt = [0] * size
        sm = [0] * size
        ans = 0

        for v in nums:
            idx = v + 1
            prev_cnt = (cnt[idx - 1] + cnt[idx + 1]) % MOD
            prev_sum = (sm[idx - 1] + sm[idx + 1]) % MOD

            new_cnt = (1 + prev_cnt) % MOD
            new_sum = (v + prev_sum + v * prev_cnt) % MOD

            ans = (ans + new_sum) % MOD

            cnt[idx] = (cnt[idx] + new_cnt) % MOD
            sm[idx] = (sm[idx] + new_sum) % MOD

        return ans

But if prev_cnt = (a+b)%MOD, new_sum = v + prev_sum + v*((a+b)%MOD). Equivalent. Good. If we didn't mod prev_cnt, new_sum = v + prev_sum + v*(a+b) mod; same. But if prev_sum = (sm_low + sm_high)%MOD, equivalent. Good.

There is a known solution:
dp = defaultdict(int)
ans = 0
for x in nums:
    c = (dp[x-1][0] + dp[x+1][0] + 1) % MOD
    s = (dp[x-1][1] + dp[x+1][1] + (dp[x-1][0] + dp[x+1][0]) * x + x) % MOD
    dp[x][0] += c
    dp[x][1] += s
    ans += s
Yes. So our solution matches.

Let's think about if there is any issue with "prev_cnt = (cnt[idx - 1] + cnt[idx + 1]) % MOD" and "new_sum = (v + prev_sum + v * prev_cnt) % MOD". Suppose prev_cnt is modulo but actual count C. v*prev_cnt mod = v*(C mod M) mod = v*C mod. Good.

Now, final code:

```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def sumOfGoodSubsequences(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        MOD = 10**9 + 7
        max_val = max(nums)
        size = max_val + 3
        cnt = [0] * size
        total = [0] * size
        ans = 0

        for v in nums:
            idx = v + 1
            prev_cnt = (cnt[idx - 1] + cnt[idx + 1]) % MOD
            prev_sum = (total[idx - 1] + total[idx + 1]) % MOD

            new_cnt = (1 + prev_cnt) % MOD
            new_sum = (v + prev_sum + v * prev_cnt) % MOD

            ans = (ans + new_sum) % MOD

            cnt[idx] = (cnt[idx] + new_cnt) % MOD
            total[idx] = (total[idx] + new_sum) % MOD

        return ans
```

Need ensure if nums is empty? Constraints non-empty. If want robust, could do if not nums: return 0. But constraints length >=1. Could include anyway. But max(nums) would error if empty. Not needed. If include, no harm. But problem says length >=1. Keep simple.

Now, final answer code block only.</think>```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def sumOfGoodSubsequences(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        MOD = 10**9 + 7

        max_val = max(nums)
        size = max_val + 3

        cnt = [0] * size
        total = [0] * size
        ans = 0

        for v in nums:
            idx = v + 1

            prev_cnt = (cnt[idx - 1] + cnt[idx + 1]) % MOD
            prev_sum = (total[idx - 1] + total[idx + 1]) % MOD

            new_cnt = (1 + prev_cnt) % MOD
            new_sum = (v + prev_sum + v * prev_cnt) % MOD

            ans = (ans + new_sum) % MOD

            cnt[idx] = (cnt[idx] + new_cnt) % MOD
            total[idx] = (total[idx] + new_sum) % MOD

        return ans
```
